The nurse acts as an advocate and witness; if the client expresses confusion or lack of understanding, the nurse must pause the consent process and notify the provider to speak with the client again.
</details>

---

### Question 128
A nurse is preparing discharge instructions for an older adult client. Which factor in the client's history places them at the highest risk for medication errors at home?

A. The client takes one daily over-the-counter multivitamin.
B. The client lives in a single-story home with their spouse.
C. The client uses a weekly pill organizer filled by a home health nurse.
D. The client is prescribed eight different routine daily medications.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D
<strong>Lens:</strong> RISK RECOGNITION (Who is at risk for injury?)
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Polypharmacy, defined as the concurrent use of multiple medications (often 5 or more), significantly increases the risk for medication errors, adverse drug interactions, and nonadherence, especially in older adults. Using a pill organizer and living with support are protective factors.
</details>

---

### Question 129
A client recently diagnosed with terminal cancer tells the nurse, "I don't want any more treatments, but my daughter is insisting I do chemotherapy." Which response by the nurse demonstrates therapeutic communication?

A. "Tell me more about how you are feeling regarding your treatment choices."
B. "You should probably listen to your daughter; she only wants what is best for you."
C. "I will tell the doctor to cancel all of your chemotherapy orders right away."
D. "Why don't you want to try the chemotherapy to see if it works first?"

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A
<strong>Lens:</strong> THERAPEUTIC COMMUNICATION (Active listening/Empathy)
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Exploring the client's feelings using an open-ended statement promotes therapeutic communication and supports the client's autonomy and right to self-determination. Asking "why" (D) can make the client defensive, offering personal advice (B) is non-therapeutic, and making assumptions to cancel orders immediately (C) skips the necessary step of exploring the client's wishes thoroughly.
</details>

---

### Question 130
The nurse is admitting a client to the medical-surgical unit who has brought a large amount of cash and several pieces of expensive jewelry. What is the nurse's first action regarding the client's valuables?

A. Place the items in a locked drawer inside the client's hospital room.
B. Encourage the client to send the valuables home with a trusted family member.
C. Document the estimated monetary value of each piece of jewelry in the chart.
D. Tape the jewelry securely to the client's body to prevent it from getting lost.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B
<strong>Lens:</strong> PROCEDURAL STEP (What is the first physical step in a skill?)
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The standard and safest procedure for managing client valuables upon admission is to encourage the client to send them home with a trusted family member. If this is not possible, the items should be counted, documented descriptively (not by estimated monetary value), and secured in the hospital safe according to facility policy. Leaving them in a bedside drawer or taping them to the client poses a severe risk of loss.
</details>
### Question 131
A registered nurse (RN) is assigning tasks for a client receiving continuous enteral tube feedings. Which task is appropriate to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP)?

A. Assessing the client's bowel sounds.
B. Flushing the feeding tube with 30 mL of water.
C. Elevating the head of the bed to at least 30 degrees.
D. Checking the residual volume of the feeding tube.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** DELEGATION
**Rationale:** The Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP) can perform basic, standard care tasks, such as elevating the head of the bed to prevent aspiration. Assessing bowel sounds, flushing the tube, and checking residual volume require nursing judgment, assessment, and sterile/clean technique, which are out of the UAP's scope of practice and must be performed by the RN or LPN.
</details>

---

### Question 132
A client is scheduled for an elective surgical procedure. The surgeon has explained the risks and benefits, but the client tells the nurse, "I still do not understand what is going to happen during the surgery." What is the nurse's legal responsibility?

A. Notify the surgeon that the client requires further explanation.
B. Explain the surgical steps using simple terms.
C. Proceed with witnessing the consent form signature.
D. Cancel the surgery and notify the nursing supervisor.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** LEGAL/ETHICAL
**Rationale:** Informed consent requires the client to fully understand the procedure, its risks, benefits, and alternatives. It is the legal responsibility of the healthcare provider performing the procedure (the surgeon) to explain it. The nurse acts as a client advocate by halting the signing process and notifying the provider that the client has unanswered questions.
</details>

---

### Question 133
The nurse is providing information about Advance Directives to a client being admitted to the hospital. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the teaching?

A. "My advance directive can only be changed by a judge."
B. "I must have a lawyer present to sign this document."
C. "This document allows my family to override my wishes."
D. "I can update or change my advance directive at any time."

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** CLIENT TEACHING
**Rationale:** An advance directive is a legal document that allows a person to outline their medical preferences or designate a healthcare proxy. A client has the right to update, change, or revoke their advance directive at any time as long as they are competent. A lawyer is not required, and families cannot arbitrarily override documented wishes.
</details>

---

### Question 134
A client tells the nurse, "I am tired of being poked with needles and want to stop all of my treatments." Which response by the nurse is the most therapeutic?

A. "You should talk to your doctor about this."
B. "Tell me more about how you are feeling regarding your treatments."
C. "It is important that you finish your treatments to get better."
D. "Your family would want you to continue with your care."

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** THERAPEUTIC COMMUNICATION
**Rationale:** In Management of Care, respecting client rights and autonomy is essential. Using an open-ended statement like "Tell me more..." encourages the client to express their feelings and concerns without judgment. The other options are dismissive, give unsolicited advice, or use guilt, all of which block therapeutic communication.
</details>

---

### Question 135
The nurse is conducting an admission assessment for an older adult client. Which finding indicates the highest risk for falls requiring immediate implementation of fall precautions?

A. The client wears reading glasses for close-up tasks.
B. The client reports a history of falling at home last week.
C. The client takes a daily multivitamin.
D. The client uses a grab bar when stepping into the shower.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** RISK RECOGNITION
**Rationale:** A recent history of falling is one of the strongest predictors of future falls. Recognizing this risk factor upon admission is a critical component of Management of Care, alerting the nurse to immediately initiate fall precautions. Using reading glasses, taking vitamins, and using a grab bar safely are normal or protective findings.
</details>
### Question 136
A nurse is working on a medical-surgical unit. A client's neighbor calls the nurse's station asking for an update on the client's condition. The client has not provided consent to share information with this neighbor. How should the nurse respond?

A) Provide a brief, general update on the client's status.
B) State, "I cannot confirm or deny whether that person is a patient here."
C) Ask the neighbor to hold while transferring the call to the client's room.
D) Tell the neighbor the client is doing well but to call back later.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Under the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) and client confidentiality laws, the nurse cannot disclose any information, including confirming or denying whether a client is admitted to the facility, without explicit permission from the client. Providing any details or transferring the call breaches confidentiality. 
**Lens:** LEGAL/ETHICAL
</details>

---

### Question 137
A registered nurse (RN) is planning assignments for the shift. Which of the following tasks is appropriate to delegate to an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A) Educating a client on how to use a standard walker.
B) Assessing a client's risk for skin breakdown using the Braden Scale.
C) Administering a scheduled oral pain medication to a postoperative client.
D) Assisting a stable, bedbound client with a routine bed bath.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Assisting a stable client with activities of daily living (ADLs), such as a bed bath, is a routine, standard task that falls within the scope of practice for a UAP. Education, assessment, and medication administration require clinical judgment and are the responsibilities of the licensed nurse.
**Lens:** DELEGATION
</details>

---

### Question 138
A client with a terminal illness tells the nurse, "I don't want any more treatments, but my daughter insists I keep fighting. I don't know what to do." Which response by the nurse is the most appropriate to support the client's rights?

A) "Let's talk about what you want and how you can share your wishes with your daughter."
B) "You should listen to your daughter; she just wants what is best for you."
C) "I will tell your doctor to cancel all your upcoming treatments immediately."
D) "Why do you want to stop fighting when there are still treatment options available?"

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Therapeutic communication involves actively listening, exploring the client's feelings, and advocating for their autonomy. This response validates the client's self-determination and offers practical support in communicating their difficult choices to their family. Avoid asking "Why" questions, giving unwanted advice, or taking action without further exploring the client's readiness.
**Lens:** THERAPEUTIC COMMUNICATION
</details>

---

### Question 139
A nurse is educating a client about advance directives upon admission to the hospital. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the teaching?

A) "My doctor is the only one who can legally make changes to my advance directive."
B) "I must have a lawyer present in the room to sign my advance directive."
C) "This document tells the healthcare team what my medical wishes are if I cannot speak for myself."
D) "Once I sign this document, I am no longer allowed to change my mind about my care."

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** An advance directive is a legal document that outlines a client's healthcare preferences (such as a living will) or designates a proxy (durable power of attorney for healthcare) in the event they become incapacitated and unable to communicate their wishes. It does not require a lawyer to sign, and the client can change or revoke it at any time as long as they are competent.
**Lens:** CLIENT TEACHING
</details>

---

### Question 140
A nurse is preparing to transfer a client from the medical-surgical unit to a long-term rehabilitation facility. What is the most appropriate initial procedural step the nurse must take to ensure continuity of care?

A) Clean the client's room so it is ready for the next admission.
B) Call the receiving facility to provide a comprehensive verbal hand-off report.
C) Discharge the client entirely from the hospital's electronic health record system.
D) Send the client's original printed medical charts in the ambulance with them.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** When transferring a client to another facility, providing a clear and thorough hand-off report to the receiving nurse is a critical procedural step. This ensures continuity of care, communicates current treatments, and maintains client safety. Original medical records stay with the discharging facility, though copies or summaries are sent.
**Lens:** PROCEDURAL STEP
</details>

# SECTION: Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies 

### Question 141
The nurse is preparing to administer ophthalmic drops to a client with glaucoma. What is the first physical step the nurse should take when instilling the medication?

A. Ask the client to look down toward the floor.
B. Gently pull down the lower eyelid to expose the conjunctival sac.
C. Wipe the eye from the outer canthus to the inner canthus.
D. Place the dropper directly against the sclera.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Gently pull down the lower eyelid to expose the conjunctival sac.

**Rationale:** When administering eye drops, the nurse should gently pull down the lower eyelid to create a small pouch (the conjunctival sac) where the drops can be safely instilled without hitting the sensitive cornea. The client should be instructed to look up, not down. The eye should be wiped from the inner to the outer canthus if cleaning is needed, and the dropper should never touch the eye to prevent injury and contamination. 
**Lens:** Procedural Step
</details>

---

### Question 142
A client receiving oral ferrous sulfate for iron deficiency anemia reports having dark, greenish-black stools for the past two days. How should the nurse interpret this finding?

A. It is a normal, expected side effect of the iron supplement.
B. It indicates an allergic reaction to the medication.
C. It is a sign of an active gastrointestinal bleed.
D. It signifies iron toxicity and requires immediate cessation of the drug.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. It is a normal, expected side effect of the iron supplement.

**Rationale:** Oral iron supplements, such as ferrous sulfate, commonly turn the stool a harmless dark green or black color. This is an expected finding and does not indicate a gastrointestinal bleed, an allergic reaction, or iron toxicity. The nurse should reassure the client that this is normal.
**Lens:** Expected vs Unexpected
</details>

---

### Question 143
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly prescribed an albuterol metered-dose inhaler (MDI). Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the teaching?

A. "I will inhale the medicine as quickly as I can."
B. "I should wash the metal canister in warm water every day."
C. "I will exhale fully before I press down on the inhaler."
D. "I need to take a deep breath before I put the mouthpiece in my mouth."

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. "I will exhale fully before I press down on the inhaler."

**Rationale:** For proper MDI use, the client should exhale completely before actuating the inhaler. Once the inhaler is pressed, the client should inhale slowly and deeply (not quickly) to ensure the medication travels deep into the lungs. The metal medication canister should never be washed in water; only the plastic mouthpiece should be rinsed.
**Lens:** Client Teaching
</details>

---

### Question 144
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous (IV) normal saline infusion. Which task is appropriate for the nurse to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Assessing the IV site for redness or swelling.
B. Adjusting the IV pump rate based on the healthcare provider's order.
C. Disconnecting the IV tubing so the client can shower.
D. Assisting the client with putting on a gown over the IV arm.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Assisting the client with putting on a gown over the IV arm.

**Rationale:** The UAP can safely assist clients with basic activities of daily living (ADLs), such as dressing and putting on a gown, while working around an existing IV line. Assessing the IV site, adjusting infusion rates, and disconnecting or manipulating IV lines are strictly nursing responsibilities and fall outside the UAP's scope of practice.
**Lens:** Delegation
</details>

---

### Question 145
The nurse is reviewing the morning laboratory results for a client taking oral warfarin for atrial fibrillation. The client's International Normalized Ratio (INR) is 2.5. What does this result indicate?

A. The dose needs to be increased because the level is too low.
B. The medication is at a therapeutic level for this condition.
C. The client is experiencing severe clotting and needs a heparin drip.
D. The dose needs to be decreased due to a high risk of bleeding.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. The medication is at a therapeutic level for this condition.

**Rationale:** A normal, healthy client not taking anticoagulants has an INR of approximately 1.0. For a client taking warfarin for conditions like atrial fibrillation, the standard therapeutic goal for the INR is between 2.0 and 3.0. An INR of 2.5 means the medication is working exactly as intended.
**Lens:** Data Interpretation
</details>
### Question 146
**Lens:** CLIENT TEACHING 
**Scenario:** A nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client who is newly prescribed levothyroxine for hypothyroidism. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to take this medication?

A) "I should take this medication with my dinner every evening."
B) "I will take this pill every morning on an empty stomach."
C) "I can stop taking it once I feel like I have more energy."
D) "I will crush the pill and mix it into a glass of milk."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Levothyroxine should be taken once daily in the morning on an empty stomach, at least 30 to 60 minutes before breakfast. Taking it with food or certain beverages (like milk) can decrease its absorption. It is a lifelong medication, and clients should not stop taking it just because their symptoms improve.<br>
<strong>NCLEX Client Need:</strong> Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies
</details>

---

### Question 147
**Lens:** PROCEDURAL STEP
**Scenario:** A nurse is preparing to insert a peripheral intravenous (IV) catheter into a client's forearm for fluid administration. What is the first physical step the nurse should take before applying the tourniquet?

A) Cleanse the selected insertion site vigorously with chlorhexidine.
B) Insert the IV catheter at a 15-degree angle.
C) Perform hand hygiene and don clean gloves.
D) Connect the primed IV tubing to the catheter hub.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Before touching the client, applying a tourniquet, or performing any invasive procedure, the nurse must first perform hand hygiene and put on clean gloves to maintain infection control. Cleansing the site, inserting the catheter, and connecting the tubing are all steps that occur after hand hygiene and gloving.<br>
<strong>NCLEX Client Need:</strong> Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies
</details>

---

### Question 148
**Lens:** CONTRAINDICATION
**Scenario:** A healthcare provider prescribes propranolol, a non-selective beta-blocker, for a client's hypertension. While reviewing the client’s medical history, the nurse identifies a condition that makes administering this drug unsafe. Which of the following conditions is a strict contraindication to this medication?

A) Asthma
B) Osteoarthritis
C) Peptic ulcer disease
D) Hyperlipidemia

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Propranolol is a non-selective beta-blocker, meaning it blocks both beta-1 receptors (in the heart) and beta-2 receptors (in the lungs). Blocking beta-2 receptors can cause bronchospasm, making it contraindicated for clients with a history of asthma or severe reactive airway disease.<br>
<strong>NCLEX Client Need:</strong> Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies
</details>

---

### Question 149
**Lens:** DATA INTERPRETATION
**Scenario:** A client is receiving a daily dose of oral warfarin for a history of atrial fibrillation. The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results and notes an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 2.5. How should the nurse interpret this finding?

A) The value is dangerously high, requiring immediate vitamin K administration.
B) The value is critically low, requiring an immediate heparin infusion.
C) The value is too low, indicating a need for a dose increase.
D) The value is within the expected therapeutic range.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> For most clients taking warfarin to prevent blood clots (such as those with atrial fibrillation), the expected therapeutic range for the INR is 2.0 to 3.0. An INR of 2.5 is perfectly within range, indicating the medication is working effectively without posing an undue bleeding risk.<br>
<strong>NCLEX Client Need:</strong> Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies
</details>

---

### Question 150
**Lens:** EVALUATION
**Scenario:** A nurse administers an albuterol nebulizer treatment to a client experiencing an acute asthma exacerbation. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the pharmacological treatment was effective?

A) The client's respiratory rate decreases from 28 to 18 breaths per minute.
B) The client's heart rate increases from 90 to 110 beats per minute.
C) The client complains of a mild, new-onset tremor in their hands.
D) The client exhibits diminished breath sounds in the lower lung lobes.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale and Details</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A<br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Albuterol is a bronchodilator used to open the airways during an asthma attack. A decrease in respiratory rate from 28 (tachypnea) to a normal rate of 18 indicates improved breathing and reduced work of breathing. An increased heart rate and hand tremors are common side effects of albuterol, not signs of therapeutic effectiveness. Diminished breath sounds would indicate worsening airway constriction.<br>
<strong>NCLEX Client Need:</strong> Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies
</details>
### Question 151
A nurse is reviewing the medication administration records of several clients on a medical-surgical unit. Based on the medications prescribed, which client is at the highest risk for bleeding complications?

A) A client taking daily acetaminophen for chronic knee osteoarthritis.
B) A client taking lisinopril for the management of primary hypertension.
C) A client taking warfarin for a history of atrial fibrillation.
D) A client taking levothyroxine for hypothyroidism.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: RISK RECOGNITION (Who is at risk for injury?)**
Warfarin is an oral anticoagulant that prevents blood clot formation. Because it alters the body's normal clotting cascade, the most significant risk associated with warfarin therapy is bleeding. The nurse must recognize this risk and monitor for signs of bruising, hematuria, or dark stools. Acetaminophen, lisinopril, and levothyroxine do not inherently place the client at a high risk for bleeding.
</details>

---

### Question 152
A client is newly prescribed simvastatin for the treatment of hyperlipidemia. Which beverage is contraindicated and should be avoided by the client while taking this medication?

A) Grapefruit juice
B) Orange juice
C) Low-fat milk
D) Apple juice

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: CONTRAINDICATION (What is unsafe to do/give?)**
Grapefruit juice is contraindicated for clients taking certain statin medications, including simvastatin. Grapefruit juice inhibits the CYP3A4 enzyme in the liver and intestines, which is responsible for metabolizing the drug. Consuming grapefruit juice can lead to toxic levels of simvastatin in the bloodstream, increasing the risk of a severe muscle condition called rhabdomyolysis. Orange juice, milk, and apple juice do not have this interaction.
</details>

---

### Question 153
A client receiving rifampin for the treatment of tuberculosis calls the clinic to report that their urine has turned a reddish-orange color. What is the nurse's best action?

A) Request a stat urinalysis and urine culture.
B) Instruct the client to hold the next dose of the medication.
C) Notify the healthcare provider immediately of this allergic reaction.
D) Document the finding and reassure the client that this is normal.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: EXPECTED VS UNEXPECTED (Is this finding normal/stable?)**
A harmless, reddish-orange discoloration of bodily fluids (including urine, sweat, saliva, and tears) is a well-known, expected side effect of rifampin. The nurse should reassure the client that this is a normal finding and does not indicate an allergy, infection, or medication toxicity. There is no need to hold the medication, notify the provider, or order a urinalysis based solely on this expected finding.
</details>

---

### Question 154
The nurse is teaching a client about the proper administration of sublingual nitroglycerin for sudden episodes of angina (chest pain). Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the basic teaching?

A) "I will swallow the pill whole with a full glass of water."
B) "I will place the tablet under my tongue and let it dissolve completely."
C) "I should chew the tablet quickly to make it work faster."
D) "I will keep the medication in the refrigerator at all times."

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: CLIENT TEACHING (Do they understand the basics?)**
Sublingual medications are designed to be absorbed directly through the highly vascular mucous membranes under the tongue. The client must allow the nitroglycerin tablet to dissolve completely under the tongue without swallowing or chewing it. Swallowing or chewing will cause the medication to be processed by the GI tract and liver, significantly delaying or destroying its effects. The medication should be kept at room temperature away from light and moisture, not in the refrigerator.
</details>

---

### Question 155
A client is taking warfarin for a history of a deep vein thrombosis (DVT). The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results and notes an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 2.5. How should the nurse interpret this basic laboratory data?

A) The level is within the expected therapeutic range for this therapy.
B) The level indicates a high risk for spontaneous, life-threatening bleeding.
C) The level is subtherapeutic and the medication dose needs to be increased.
D) The level requires the immediate administration of vitamin K as an antidote.

<details>
<summary><strong>Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: DATA INTERPRETATION (What does this basic lab mean?)**
The expected therapeutic INR range for a client taking warfarin for a DVT is typically between 2.0 and 3.0. An INR of 2.5 is right in the middle of this safe and effective range. Therefore, the nurse interprets this as a stable, normal finding for this specific therapy. It does not indicate a high risk for bleeding, it is not subtherapeutic, and it does not require vitamin K (which is given for dangerously high INRs or active bleeding).
</details>
### Question 156
**Lens:** CLIENT TEACHING (Do they understand the basics?)
**Topic:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies

A nurse is teaching a client about taking sublingual nitroglycerin for sudden chest pain. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the basic medication instructions?

A) "I should swallow the pill with a full glass of water."
B) "I should keep the pills in a clear, plastic bag in my pocket."
C) "I will place the tablet under my tongue and let it dissolve."
D) "I can chew the tablet if the pain is very severe."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Client Teaching (Assessing the client's grasp of basic medication administration)

**Rationale:** Sublingual medications must be placed under the tongue to dissolve and be absorbed directly through the mucous membranes into the bloodstream. Swallowing or chewing alters the absorption and reduces the medication's effectiveness. Nitroglycerin must also be kept in its original dark container, as light degrades the medication, so keeping it in a clear plastic bag is incorrect.
</details>

---

### Question 157
**Lens:** PROCEDURAL STEP (What is the first physical step in a skill?)
**Topic:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies

The nurse is preparing to administer an intravenous (IV) piggyback antibiotic to a client. After verifying the provider's orders and gathering the supplies, what is the first physical step the nurse should take before handling the IV tubing and medication bag?

A) Perform hand hygiene.
B) Prime the new IV tubing.
C) Scrub the hub of the primary IV line.
D) Hang the piggyback bag higher than the primary bag.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Procedural Step (Identifying the fundamental physical start of a clinical skill)

**Rationale:** The first physical step in any medication administration or IV procedure, prior to touching the equipment or the patient, is to perform hand hygiene. This is a basic standard precaution to prevent the spread of infection. Priming the tubing, scrubbing the hub, and positioning the bags are all subsequent steps.
</details>

---

### Question 158
**Lens:** DATA INTERPRETATION (What does this basic lab mean?)
**Topic:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies

A client is receiving a daily dose of oral warfarin to prevent blood clots. The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results and notes an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 2.5. How should the nurse interpret this basic laboratory result?

A) The INR indicates that the warfarin dose needs to be doubled.
B) The INR is critically high, and the client needs an antidote.
C) The INR is dangerously low, indicating active bleeding.
D) The INR is within the expected therapeutic range.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Data Interpretation (Understanding the meaning of basic lab values related to medications)

**Rationale:** For most clients taking warfarin, the expected therapeutic INR range is 2.0 to 3.0. An INR of 2.5 is a normal, stable finding, indicating that the medication is working effectively to prevent blood clots without placing the client at an excessive risk for bleeding.
</details>

---

### Question 159
**Lens:** CONTRAINDICATION (What is unsafe to do/give?)
**Topic:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies

The nurse is preparing to administer intravenous (IV) potassium chloride to a client diagnosed with hypokalemia. Which method of administration is strictly contraindicated and unsafe for this medication?

A) Diluting the medication in a primary IV fluid bag.
B) Administering the medication as an undiluted, rapid IV push.
C) Infusing the medication slowly using an electronic infusion pump.
D) Checking the client's serum potassium level before administration.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Contraindication (Recognizing highly unsafe practices with specific medications)

**Rationale:** Intravenous potassium chloride must *never* be administered by IV push or as an undiluted bolus. Doing so is strictly contraindicated because a rapid influx of potassium can cause immediate and fatal cardiac arrhythmias. It must always be appropriately diluted and administered slowly via an IV infusion pump.
</details>

---

### Question 160
**Lens:** EVALUATION (Is the patient stable after care?)
**Topic:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies

A client with asthma is administered an albuterol nebulizer treatment for shortness of breath and wheezing. Thirty minutes later, the nurse assesses the client to evaluate the effectiveness of the medication. Which finding indicates the client is stable and the medication was effective?

A) The client's respiratory rate is 16 breaths per minute with clear lung sounds.
B) The client reports an increase in chest tightness.
C) The client is actively using accessory muscles to breathe.
D) The client has a continuous, highly audible inspiratory wheeze.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens Explanation</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Evaluation (Determining if the pharmacological intervention achieved the desired outcome)

**Rationale:** Albuterol is a bronchodilator used to open the airways and relieve bronchospasms. An effective outcome is demonstrated by unlabored breathing (a normal respiratory rate) and clear lung sounds, indicating that the wheezing and shortness of breath have successfully resolved. The other options indicate worsening or unresolved symptoms.
</details>
### Question 161
**Lens:** RISK RECOGNITION

A nurse is reviewing the medication list of a client scheduled for major abdominal surgery early tomorrow morning. Which of the client's daily medications places them at the highest risk for surgical complications?

A) Acetaminophen  
B) Daily multivitamin  
C) Warfarin  
D) Levothyroxine  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C<br>
Warfarin is an oral anticoagulant that prevents blood clots. Taking it prior to surgery places the client at a high risk for excessive and potentially life-threatening bleeding during and after the procedure. Clients are typically instructed to stop warfarin several days before surgery under the guidance of a healthcare provider. Acetaminophen, multivitamins, and levothyroxine generally do not pose a severe bleeding risk.
</details>

---

### Question 162
**Lens:** CONTRAINDICATION

The nurse is preparing to administer medications to a client with a history of severe asthma. Which medication prescription should the nurse question as potentially unsafe for this client?

A) Albuterol  
B) Acetaminophen  
C) Guaifenesin  
D) Propranolol  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D<br>
Propranolol is a non-selective beta-blocker. While it lowers heart rate and blood pressure by blocking beta-1 receptors, it also blocks beta-2 receptors in the lungs, which can cause severe bronchoconstriction. This is strictly contraindicated in clients with asthma. Albuterol is a bronchodilator used to treat asthma, while acetaminophen and guaifenesin are generally safe to use.
</details>

---

### Question 163
**Lens:** EXPECTED VS UNEXPECTED

A client receiving an intravenous (IV) infusion of 0.9% sodium chloride complains of mild discomfort at the insertion site. The nurse assesses the site and observes that it is swollen, cool to the touch, and pale. What does this finding indicate?

A) Phlebitis  
B) Infiltration  
C) Expected response to room-temperature IV fluids  
D) Systemic infection  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B<br>
Swelling, pallor, and coolness at an IV insertion site are unexpected findings that indicate infiltration. This occurs when the IV catheter slips out of the vein and the non-vesicant fluid leaks into the surrounding subcutaneous tissue. Phlebitis (inflammation of the vein) typically presents with warmth, redness, and a red streak along the vein.
</details>

---

### Question 164
**Lens:** CLIENT TEACHING

A client is prescribed a fluticasone inhaler for the daily management of asthma. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to use this medication?

A) "I should rinse my mouth with water and spit it out after using the inhaler."  
B) "I will use this inhaler immediately if I start having an acute asthma attack."  
C) "I can stop taking this medication as soon as my breathing improves."  
D) "I should swallow the water after rinsing my mouth to make sure I get all the medicine."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A<br>
Fluticasone is an inhaled corticosteroid used for long-term control of asthma. Rinsing the mouth and spitting after use removes residual medication from the oral cavity, significantly reducing the risk of developing oral candidiasis (thrush). Corticosteroids are not rescue inhalers (like albuterol) and should not be used for acute attacks. The medication must be taken daily, even when feeling well, to prevent airway inflammation.
</details>

---

### Question 165
**Lens:** DATA INTERPRETATION

A client is taking digoxin for heart failure. The morning laboratory results show a serum potassium level of 2.9 mEq/L (2.9 mmol/L). What is the nurse's best interpretation of this finding related to the client's medication?

A) The low potassium level increases the client's risk for digoxin toxicity.  
B) The potassium level is normal and safe for scheduled digoxin administration.  
C) The potassium level is elevated and will decrease the effectiveness of the digoxin.  
D) Digoxin normally causes an expected decrease in serum potassium levels.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A<br>
The normal serum potassium level is 3.5 to 5.0 mEq/L. A level of 2.9 mEq/L indicates hypokalemia. Low potassium levels sensitize the heart muscle to digoxin, greatly increasing the risk of life-threatening digoxin toxicity and dysrhythmias, even if the digoxin level itself is within a normal therapeutic range. The nurse should hold the medication and notify the healthcare provider.
</details>
### Question 166
The nurse is preparing to administer a rapid intravenous (IV) fluid bolus of 0.9% sodium chloride to several clients. Which client is at the highest risk for developing fluid volume overload from this therapy? (Lens: RISK RECOGNITION)

A. A client experiencing a sickle cell crisis  
B. A client with a documented history of heart failure  
C. A client admitted with severe, acute gastroenteritis  
D. A client with a high fever and diaphoresis  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
Clients with heart failure have impaired cardiac pumping capacity and are highly susceptible to fluid volume overload. A rapid IV fluid bolus can quickly back up into the lungs, causing pulmonary edema. The other clients (sickle cell crisis, gastroenteritis, high fever) are typically dehydrated and would benefit from, and generally tolerate, IV fluid hydration well without the same high risk of overload.
</details>

---

### Question 167
The nurse is reviewing the medication administration record (MAR) for a client newly prescribed oral propranolol. Which condition in the client's medical history would make this medication unsafe to administer? (Lens: CONTRAINDICATION)

A. Essential hypertension  
B. Frequent migraine headaches  
C. History of asthma  
D. Sinus tachycardia  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
Propranolol is a non-selective beta-blocker. Because it blocks both beta-1 (heart) and beta-2 (lungs) receptors, it can cause severe bronchoconstriction and is contraindicated in clients with asthma or chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). It is commonly indicated for treating hypertension, migraines, and tachycardia. 
</details>

---

### Question 168
A client taking oral ferrous sulfate (iron) reports to the nurse that their recent bowel movements have been dark green-black. How should the nurse interpret this finding? (Lens: EXPECTED VS UNEXPECTED)

A. It indicates upper gastrointestinal bleeding.  
B. It suggests the dose of the medication is too high.  
C. It is a sign of an allergic reaction to the supplement.  
D. It is an expected, harmless effect of the medication.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
Oral iron supplements commonly cause stools to turn dark green or black. This is a normal, expected, and harmless side effect of the medication. The nurse should reassure the client that this does not indicate bleeding or an allergic reaction, provided there are no other symptoms of bleeding (like tarry consistency or abdominal pain).
</details>

---

### Question 169
The nurse provides teaching to a client prescribed sublingual nitroglycerin for angina. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the medication instructions? (Lens: CLIENT TEACHING)

A. "I should place the tablet under my tongue and let it dissolve."  
B. "I will chew the tablet quickly if my chest pain is severe."  
C. "I must swallow the pill with a full glass of water."  
D. "I can swallow the tablet whole if I don't like the taste."  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
Sublingual nitroglycerin must be placed under the tongue and allowed to completely dissolve. This allows the medication to be rapidly absorbed directly into the systemic circulation through the oral mucosa. Swallowing or chewing the tablet destroys its rapid-acting properties due to the first-pass metabolism in the liver. 
</details>

---

### Question 170
A client is receiving daily doses of warfarin for a history of a deep vein thrombosis (DVT). The nurse notes today's International Normalized Ratio (INR) is 2.5. What does this laboratory result indicate? (Lens: DATA INTERPRETATION)

A. The blood is clotting too quickly and requires intervention.  
B. The client is at a critically high risk for spontaneous bleeding.  
C. The medication is currently at a safe, therapeutic level.  
D. The dosage of the medication needs to be significantly increased.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
The normal, unmedicated INR is approximately 1.0. For a client receiving warfarin therapy to treat or prevent a deep vein thrombosis (DVT), the expected therapeutic INR range is typically 2.0 to 3.0. An INR of 2.5 falls perfectly within this range, indicating that the medication is effective and at a safe level.
</details>
### Question 171
The nurse is reviewing the medication administration records of several clients. Which client is at the highest risk for respiratory depression when receiving intravenous morphine?

A) A 25-year-old with a fractured femur.
B) A 45-year-old with a history of primary hypertension.
C) A 78-year-old with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD).
D) A 30-year-old recovering from an uncomplicated appendectomy.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) A 78-year-old with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD).

**Reasoning:** 
**Lens: Risk Recognition**
Morphine is an opioid analgesic that causes central nervous system depression, which can significantly decrease the respiratory rate. Older adults and clients with underlying respiratory conditions, such as COPD, asthma, or sleep apnea, are at a much higher risk for severe respiratory depression when receiving opioids. The other clients listed do not have identified risk factors that make them uniquely vulnerable to the respiratory depressant effects of morphine.
</details>

---

### Question 172
The provider prescribes intravenous potassium chloride for a client with hypokalemia. Which method of administration is strictly contraindicated and unsafe for the nurse to perform?

A) Administering the medication via a central venous catheter.
B) Administering the medication as a rapid intravenous push.
C) Diluting the medication in a 1-liter bag of normal saline.
D) Using an electronic infusion pump to deliver the medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Administering the medication as a rapid intravenous push.

**Reasoning:** 
**Lens: Contraindication**
Intravenous potassium chloride must **never** be administered by IV push, bolus, or in an undiluted form. Giving potassium rapidly can cause immediate and fatal cardiac dysrhythmias, including cardiac arrest. It must always be diluted and administered slowly using an electronic infusion pump. Central lines are often preferred for higher concentrations because potassium can be irritating to peripheral veins.
</details>

---

### Question 173
A client is receiving oral rifampin for the treatment of tuberculosis. The client reports to the nurse that their urine has turned a reddish-orange color. How should the nurse interpret this finding?

A) An expected, harmless side effect of the medication.
B) A sign of acute kidney injury requiring immediate intervention.
C) An indication that the medication dosage is too low.
D) A symptom of a severe allergic reaction to the drug.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) An expected, harmless side effect of the medication.

**Reasoning:** 
**Lens: Expected vs Unexpected**
Rifampin is an antitubercular medication known to cause a harmless reddish-orange discoloration of bodily fluids, including urine, sweat, saliva, and tears. This is a normal, expected finding, and the nurse should reassure the client. It is not a sign of kidney injury, incorrect dosage, or an allergic reaction.
</details>

---

### Question 174
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client who has a new prescription for sublingual nitroglycerin to treat angina. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to take this medication?

A) "I will swallow the pill whole with a large glass of water."
B) "I should chew the tablet quickly as soon as chest pain starts."
C) "I need to take this medication every morning with breakfast."
D) "I will place the tablet under my tongue and let it dissolve."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) "I will place the tablet under my tongue and let it dissolve."

**Reasoning:** 
**Lens: Client Teaching**
Sublingual medications, like nitroglycerin for acute angina, must be placed under the tongue and allowed to dissolve completely. The rich blood supply under the tongue allows for rapid absorption directly into the bloodstream. It should not be swallowed or chewed, as the gastrointestinal tract will break it down and drastically reduce its effectiveness. It is taken as needed for chest pain, not as a daily routine medication with breakfast.
</details>

---

### Question 175
A client is receiving daily doses of oral warfarin for the treatment of a deep vein thrombosis (DVT). The nurse notes that the client's most recent International Normalized Ratio (INR) is 2.5. How should the nurse interpret this laboratory result?

A) The INR is dangerously high, indicating a severe risk of bleeding.
B) The INR is within the expected therapeutic range for this condition.
C) The INR is subtherapeutic, meaning the warfarin dose needs to be increased.
D) The INR cannot be interpreted without knowing the client's platelet count.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) The INR is within the expected therapeutic range for this condition.

**Reasoning:** 
**Lens: Data Interpretation**
For most clients taking warfarin to treat or prevent a deep vein thrombosis (DVT) or atrial fibrillation, the target therapeutic INR range is between 2.0 and 3.0. A result of 2.5 indicates that the blood is clotting at an appropriate, safe rate to prevent further clots without causing an excessive risk of bleeding. Platelet counts are used to monitor heparin therapy, not warfarin therapy.
</details>
### Question 176
**Lens:** RISK RECOGNITION
A nurse is reviewing the medication profile for a client diagnosed with heart failure who is taking digoxin. Which additional medication puts the client at the highest risk for developing digoxin toxicity?

A) A daily multivitamin supplement
B) A potassium-sparing diuretic, such as spironolactone
C) A loop diuretic, such as furosemide
D) A short-acting bronchodilator, such as albuterol

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Rationale:** Loop diuretics like furosemide cause the kidneys to excrete potassium, leading to hypokalemia. Low potassium levels significantly increase the risk of digoxin toxicity, a potentially life-threatening condition. Potassium-sparing diuretics (Option B) retain potassium and are less likely to cause this specific interaction, though hyperkalemia must be monitored.
</details>

---

### Question 177
**Lens:** CONTRAINDICATION
A nurse is preparing to administer prescribed antibiotics to a client. The client’s chart clearly states a history of anaphylaxis to penicillin. Which of the following medications is absolutely contraindicated for this client?

A) Ampicillin
B) Azithromycin
C) Levofloxacin
D) Doxycycline

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A  
**Rationale:** Ampicillin is a penicillin-class antibiotic. Administering it to a client with a known severe allergy (anaphylaxis) to penicillin is strictly contraindicated and could lead to a fatal allergic reaction. Azithromycin (macrolide), levofloxacin (fluoroquinolone), and doxycycline (tetracycline) belong to different drug classes and do not carry the same cross-reactivity risk.
</details>

---

### Question 178
**Lens:** EXPECTED VS UNEXPECTED
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous (IV) infusion of normal saline. While assessing the IV insertion site, the nurse notes that the surrounding skin is cool to the touch, pale, and noticeably swollen. How should the nurse interpret this finding?

A) This is an expected localized healing response to the catheter.
B) The client is developing superficial phlebitis.
C) The client is developing a localized bacterial infection.
D) The IV fluid is infiltrating into the surrounding tissue.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D  
**Rationale:** Coolness, pallor, and swelling at an IV site are classic, unexpected signs of infiltration, which occurs when IV fluid leaks into the surrounding subcutaneous tissue instead of the vein. Phlebitis (Option B) typically presents with warmth, redness, and a palpable cord. Infection (Option C) would present with warmth, redness, and possibly purulent drainage. 
</details>

---

### Question 179
**Lens:** CLIENT TEACHING
A nurse is educating a client who has a new prescription for sublingual nitroglycerin to treat angina (chest pain). Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to take this medication?

A) "I will swallow the pill whole with a full glass of water."
B) "I should place the tablet under my tongue and let it dissolve."
C) "I can chew the tablet if my chest pain is very severe."
D) "I will crush the tablet and mix it into a spoonful of applesauce."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Rationale:** Sublingual medications, such as nitroglycerin, are designed to be absorbed rapidly through the highly vascular mucous membranes under the tongue. Swallowing, chewing, or crushing the tablet destroys its rapid-acting properties and subjects it to the first-pass effect in the liver, rendering it ineffective for sudden chest pain.
</details>

---

### Question 180
**Lens:** DATA INTERPRETATION
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion for the treatment of a deep vein thrombosis (DVT). The nurse checks the client’s morning laboratory results. Which activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) value indicates a safe and therapeutic response to the therapy?

A) 20 seconds
B) 70 seconds
C) 35 seconds
D) 150 seconds

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Rationale:** The normal aPTT range is approximately 30 to 40 seconds. For a client on continuous heparin therapy, the therapeutic goal is typically 1.5 to 2.5 times the normal baseline (roughly 60 to 80 seconds). An aPTT of 70 seconds is therapeutic. An aPTT of 20 or 35 seconds is too low (subtherapeutic), and 150 seconds is dangerously high, placing the client at severe risk for bleeding.
</details>
### Question 181
The nurse is reviewing the medication records of four assigned clients. Which client is at the greatest risk for experiencing bleeding complications?

A) A client taking daily over-the-counter acetaminophen for knee osteoarthritis.
B) A client taking a daily lisinopril tablet for the management of hypertension.
C) A client taking prescribed warfarin and frequent doses of over-the-counter aspirin.
D) A client taking oral diphenhydramine as needed for seasonal allergies.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> RISK RECOGNITION (Who is at risk for injury?) <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Warfarin is a prescribed anticoagulant, and aspirin has antiplatelet properties. Taking these medications concurrently significantly increases the client's risk for bleeding complications. Acetaminophen, lisinopril, and diphenhydramine do not typically increase bleeding risk.
</details>

---

### Question 182
The nurse is preparing to administer ordered potassium chloride to a client diagnosed with severe hypokalemia. Which route of administration is strictly unsafe and contraindicated for this medication?

A) Intravenous push (IVP)
B) Intravenous piggyback (IVPB)
C) Oral liquid suspension
D) Enteral feeding tube

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> CONTRAINDICATION (What is unsafe to do/give?) <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Potassium chloride must never be administered by intravenous push (IVP) or rapid IV bolus because it can cause fatal cardiac dysrhythmias and immediate cardiac arrest. It must always be appropriately diluted and given via a slow, controlled intravenous infusion, or given orally/enterally.
</details>

---

### Question 183
A client receiving an oral ferrous sulfate supplement for iron deficiency anemia reports having dark, greenish-black stools for the past two days. How should the nurse interpret this finding?

A) A sign of active gastrointestinal bleeding requiring immediate intervention.
B) An indication of liver toxicity resulting from the iron supplement.
C) An allergic reaction to the enteric coating of the ferrous sulfate tablet.
D) An expected and harmless side effect of the iron medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> EXPECTED VS UNEXPECTED (Is this finding normal/stable?) <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Oral iron supplements, such as ferrous sulfate, commonly turn a client's stool a dark green or black color. This is a completely normal, expected, and harmless side effect of the therapy and does not indicate gastrointestinal bleeding or toxicity. 
</details>

---

### Question 184
The nurse is teaching a client how to properly use a prescribed nitroglycerin transdermal patch for angina. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of the basics?

A) "I should apply the new patch directly over the exact same spot as the old one."
B) "I will remove the old patch before applying a new one."
C) "I can cut the patch in half if my chest pain is only mild today."
D) "I will leave the patch on for 24 hours a day without taking a break."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> CLIENT TEACHING (Do they understand the basics?) <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The client must remove the old nitroglycerin patch before applying a new one to prevent medication overdose and severe hypotension. Patches should never be cut, application sites should be rotated to prevent skin irritation, and a patch-free period (usually 10-12 hours at night) is required to prevent the body from building a tolerance to the medication.
</details>

---

### Question 185
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion for a deep vein thrombosis. The morning laboratory results show an activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) of 65 seconds. The normal control value is 30 seconds. What does this basic laboratory result mean for the client?

A) The heparin dose is too low and the client is at high risk for further clotting.
B) The heparin dose is therapeutic and within the expected target range.
C) The heparin dose is extremely toxic and the infusion should be stopped immediately.
D) The client is showing signs of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B <br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> DATA INTERPRETATION (What does this basic lab mean?) <br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> The therapeutic target range for aPTT when a client is on a continuous heparin infusion is typically 1.5 to 2.5 times the normal control value. With a control of 30 seconds, the therapeutic range is 45 to 75 seconds. An aPTT of 65 seconds indicates that the medication is working properly and is within the safe, expected therapeutic range.
</details>
### Question 186
**Lens: CONTRAINDICATION (What is unsafe to do/give?)**

The nurse is reviewing the chart of a client who has been prescribed oral ibuprofen for mild musculoskeletal pain. Which finding in the client’s medical history makes it unsafe to administer this medication?

A. History of mild osteoarthritis
B. Active peptic ulcer disease
C. Allergy to penicillin antibiotics
D. Resting pulse rate of 88 beats per minute

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** B. Active peptic ulcer disease
**Rationale:** Ibuprofen is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID). NSAIDs can cause gastrointestinal irritation and bleeding, making them unsafe and contraindicated for clients with active peptic ulcer disease. Osteoarthritis is an indication for NSAIDs. Penicillin allergies and a normal resting pulse rate do not contraindicate the use of ibuprofen.
</details>

---

### Question 187
**Lens: CLIENT TEACHING (Do they understand the basics?)**

The nurse is teaching a client about the safe use and storage of sublingual nitroglycerin tablets for stable angina. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the instructions?

A. "I will swallow the pill with a full glass of water."
B. "I will chew the pill if my chest hurts really badly."
C. "I need to store the bottle in the refrigerator at all times."
D. "I should keep the pills in the original dark glass bottle."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** D. "I should keep the pills in the original dark glass bottle."
**Rationale:** Sublingual nitroglycerin is sensitive to light, heat, and moisture, so it must be kept in its original dark glass bottle to maintain its effectiveness. It should not be refrigerated as condensation can introduce moisture. Sublingual tablets must be placed under the tongue to dissolve, not swallowed or chewed, as they rely on rapid absorption through the oral mucosa.
</details>

---

### Question 188
**Lens: DATA INTERPRETATION (What does this basic lab mean?)**

A client is receiving a continuous intravenous (IV) infusion of heparin for the treatment of a deep vein thrombosis. Which laboratory value should the nurse monitor to evaluate the therapeutic effectiveness of this medication?

A. Activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT)
B. Prothrombin time (PT)
C. International normalized ratio (INR)
D. Serum potassium level

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** A. Activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT)
**Rationale:** The aPTT is the specific laboratory test used to monitor the therapeutic effect of continuous IV heparin. PT and INR are used to monitor the effectiveness of warfarin therapy. Serum potassium is not directly related to heparin therapy, though electrolytes are monitored generally.
</details>

---

### Question 189
**Lens: DELEGATION (What is safe to give to a UAP?)**

A client is receiving continuous intravenous (IV) fluids through a peripheral catheter in their right forearm. Which task related to the client's IV therapy is safe and appropriate for the nurse to delegate to the unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP)?

A. Flushing the IV catheter with normal saline
B. Regulating the flow rate on the IV pump
C. Reporting if the IV dressing becomes wet or loose
D. Assessing the IV site for signs of phlebitis or infiltration

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** C. Reporting if the IV dressing becomes wet or loose
**Rationale:** UAPs can perform basic observational tasks and report findings to the nurse, such as noticing a loose or wet dressing. Assessing the site, regulating IV pumps, and flushing IV catheters all require nursing judgment, specialized training, and a nursing license, and therefore cannot be delegated to a UAP.
</details>

---

### Question 190
**Lens: PROCEDURAL STEP (What is the first physical step in a skill?)**

The nurse is preparing to administer prescribed ophthalmic (eye) drops to a client. After performing hand hygiene and verifying the medication, what is the correct physical step the nurse should take before instilling the drops?

A. Instruct the client to look down toward the floor.
B. Drop the medication directly onto the center of the cornea.
C. Clean the eyelids and eyelashes, wiping from the inner to outer canthus.
D. Ask the client to squeeze their eyes shut tightly after the drops are given.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** C. Clean the eyelids and eyelashes, wiping from the inner to outer canthus.
**Rationale:** Before administering eye drops, the nurse should ensure the eye is clean of drainage by wiping from the inner canthus (near the nose) to the outer canthus to prevent contamination of the tear duct. The client should look up (not down), the medication should be dropped into the lower conjunctival sac (not directly on the sensitive cornea), and the client should close their eyes gently (not squeeze tightly, which forces the medication out).
</details>
### Question 191
A nurse is reviewing the medication history of a client. Which client is at the highest risk for hepatotoxicity when taking over-the-counter acetaminophen?

A. A client who consumes 3 to 4 alcoholic beverages daily.
B. A client who has a history of peptic ulcer disease.
C. A client who takes daily aspirin for heart health.
D. A client who has a history of chronic kidney disease.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**
**Lens: RISK RECOGNITION (Who is at risk for injury?)**

Acetaminophen is metabolized primarily by the liver. Regular and heavy consumption of alcohol significantly increases the risk of severe liver damage (hepatotoxicity) when taking acetaminophen, even at normal doses. Peptic ulcer disease is a risk factor for NSAID use, not acetaminophen. Taking daily aspirin does not inherently increase acetaminophen hepatotoxicity, and while chronic kidney disease affects the excretion of many drugs, the primary, immediate risk for acetaminophen toxicity is hepatic impairment from alcohol use. 
</details>

---

### Question 192
A client is prescribed oral cephalexin for a bacterial skin infection. Which finding in the client's medical record is a contraindication and would prompt the nurse to withhold the medication?

A. A documented allergy to sulfonamides.
B. A history of anaphylaxis to penicillin.
C. A history of mild nausea when taking erythromycin.
D. A documented allergy to natural rubber latex.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**
**Lens: CONTRAINDICATION (What is unsafe to do/give?)**

Cephalosporins (such as cephalexin) have a structural similarity to penicillins. A history of a severe, life-threatening allergic reaction (anaphylaxis) to penicillin is a strict contraindication to receiving cephalosporins due to the risk of cross-reactivity. Allergies to sulfonamides or latex do not contraindicate cephalexin. Mild nausea with erythromycin is a common side effect, not a true allergy, and does not relate to cephalosporins.
</details>

---

### Question 193
A client taking an oral ferrous sulfate supplement for anemia reports having dark, greenish-black stools for the past two days. How should the nurse interpret this finding?

A. It indicates an acute upper gastrointestinal bleed.
B. It is an unexpected sign of iron toxicity.
C. It is an expected side effect of the iron supplement.
D. It suggests the iron is not being absorbed properly.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**
**Lens: EXPECTED VS UNEXPECTED (Is this finding normal/stable?)**

Dark, greenish-black stools are a harmless, expected side effect of oral iron therapy (ferrous sulfate). The client does not need to stop the medication. While black, tarry stools can sometimes indicate an upper gastrointestinal bleed, in the context of an iron supplement, this is an expected and benign finding. It does not indicate toxicity or malabsorption.
</details>

---

### Question 194
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client with a new prescription for sublingual nitroglycerin for angina. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the correct administration technique?

A. "I will swallow the tablet with a full glass of water."
B. "I should keep the tablets in the clear plastic pillbox in my purse."
C. "I will chew the tablet quickly if I feel chest pain starting."
D. "I should place the tablet under my tongue and let it dissolve."

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D**
**Lens: CLIENT TEACHING (Do they understand the basics?)**

Sublingual nitroglycerin must be placed under the tongue and allowed to dissolve completely for rapid absorption directly into the bloodstream. It should never be swallowed or chewed, as the gastrointestinal tract and liver will break it down before it can be effective (first-pass effect). Nitroglycerin must also be kept in its original dark container, as it degrades when exposed to light, making a clear plastic pillbox inappropriate.
</details>

---

### Question 195
A client receiving long-term oral warfarin therapy for atrial fibrillation has routine blood work drawn. The nurse reviews the laboratory results and notes an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 2.5. What is the nurse's best interpretation of this result?

A. The level is within the expected therapeutic range.
B. The level is subtherapeutic, and the dose needs to be increased.
C. The level is dangerously high, and vitamin K should be administered.
D. The level indicates active bleeding, and the provider must be notified.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**
**Lens: DATA INTERPRETATION (What does this basic lab mean?)**

The expected therapeutic INR range for a client taking warfarin for atrial fibrillation is generally between 2.0 and 3.0. An INR of 2.5 falls perfectly within this range, indicating that the client's blood is appropriately anticoagulated. Therefore, the level is neither subtherapeutic nor dangerously high, and no reversal agent (vitamin K) or dose adjustment is needed based on this value alone.
</details>
### Question 196
A nurse is reviewing the medication list of a client who takes digoxin daily for heart failure. Which concurrent medication places this client at the highest risk for developing digoxin toxicity?

A) Acetaminophen
B) Pantoprazole
C) Furosemide
D) Levothyroxine

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Lens:** RISK RECOGNITION (Who is at risk for injury?)

**Rationale:** Furosemide is a potassium-wasting loop diuretic. Hypokalemia (low potassium) greatly increases the risk of digoxin toxicity, which can cause severe arrhythmias, nausea, and visual changes. Acetaminophen, pantoprazole, and levothyroxine do not typically cause hypokalemia or increase the risk of digoxin toxicity.
</details>

---

### Question 197
A nurse is teaching a client how to self-administer sublingual nitroglycerin for sudden chest pain. Which statement by the client indicates an understanding of the basic medication instructions?

A) "I will chew the tablet before swallowing it to make it work faster."
B) "I should keep the medication in its original dark glass bottle."
C) "I will drink a full glass of water immediately after placing it under my tongue."
D) "I should store the tablets in my shirt pocket so they are always close by."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Lens:** CLIENT TEACHING (Do they understand the basics?)

**Rationale:** Nitroglycerin is highly sensitive to light, heat, and moisture. It must be kept in its original dark glass bottle to prevent it from losing its potency. It should be placed under the tongue to dissolve and absorb systemically, not chewed or swallowed. Drinking water immediately can wash it away, and keeping it in a pocket exposes the medication to body heat, which can degrade it.
</details>

---

### Question 198
A client is receiving continuous warfarin therapy for atrial fibrillation. The nurse reviews the client's morning laboratory results and notes an International Normalized Ratio (INR) of 2.5. How should the nurse interpret this finding?

A) The level is subtherapeutic, and the warfarin dose needs to be increased.
B) The level is critically high, and the nurse should prepare to administer vitamin K.
C) The level indicates the client is experiencing severe active internal bleeding.
D) The level is within the expected therapeutic range for this condition.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Lens:** DATA INTERPRETATION (What does this basic lab mean?)

**Rationale:** For a client taking warfarin for atrial fibrillation, the expected therapeutic INR range is typically between 2.0 and 3.0. An INR of 2.5 is right in the middle of this safe and effective range. It is not subtherapeutic, critically high, or indicative of bleeding.
</details>

---

### Question 199
A nurse administers an albuterol nebulizer treatment to a client experiencing an asthma exacerbation. Which assessment finding best indicates to the nurse that the pharmacological treatment was effective?

A) The client's respiratory rate decreases from 28 to 18 breaths per minute.
B) The client exhibits fine tremors in both hands.
C) The client's heart rate increases from 90 to 112 beats per minute.
D) The client reports a persistent dry mouth and throat.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Lens:** EVALUATION (Is the patient stable after care?)

**Rationale:** Albuterol is a short-acting bronchodilator used to open the airways and ease breathing. A decrease in respiratory rate from 28 (tachypnea) to 18 (normal) indicates a reduction in the work of breathing and successful treatment. Tremors, an increased heart rate (tachycardia), and dry mouth are common expected side effects of albuterol, but they do not measure the therapeutic effectiveness of the drug.
</details>

---

### Question 200
A nurse is preparing to draw up a prescribed medication from a glass ampule. What is the correct first physical step the nurse should take?

A) Snap the neck of the ampule vigorously toward the body.
B) Tap the top of the ampule lightly to move fluid down into the base.
C) Inject an amount of air equal to the medication volume into the ampule.
D) Withdraw the medication using a standard large-bore injection needle.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Lens:** PROCEDURAL STEP (What is the first physical step in a skill?)

**Rationale:** Before opening a glass ampule, the nurse must first tap the top (the stem) lightly to ensure all the medication drops down into the base, preventing spills when it is opened. The ampule should be broken away from the body to prevent injury. Air is never injected into an ampule because it lacks a rubber seal and is an open system once broken. Finally, a filter needle—not a standard needle—must be used to withdraw the medication to prevent drawing up microscopic glass shards.
</details>
### Question 201
A nurse is reviewing the medical records of four clients on a medical-surgical unit. Which client is at the highest risk for developing an adverse bleeding event while taking prescribed oral warfarin?

A) A client who has a documented history of severe gastrointestinal ulcers.
B) A client who consumes a consistent daily amount of leafy green vegetables.
C) A client who takes prescribed acetaminophen for occasional tension headaches.
D) A client who wears daily compression stockings for chronic venous insufficiency.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> A

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens 1: RISK RECOGNITION</strong>
Warfarin is an oral anticoagulant that increases the risk of bleeding. A client with a history of severe gastrointestinal ulcers has a structural vulnerability in the GI tract, placing them at the highest risk for potentially life-threatening internal bleeding while on this therapy. Consuming consistent amounts of leafy greens (Vitamin K) is actually recommended to keep INR levels stable. Acetaminophen is generally safer than NSAIDs for clients on warfarin, and compression stockings carry no bleeding risk.
</details>

---

### Question 202
A nurse is preparing to administer ibuprofen to a client reporting moderate joint pain. Which condition in the client's medical history is a clear contraindication to receiving this medication?

A) Osteoarthritis
B) Active peptic ulcer disease
C) Mild essential hypertension
D) Type 2 diabetes mellitus

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> B

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens 2: CONTRAINDICATION</strong>
Ibuprofen is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID). NSAIDs inhibit prostaglandins that protect the stomach lining, which can cause or exacerbate gastrointestinal bleeding. Therefore, ibuprofen is strictly contraindicated in clients with active peptic ulcer disease. It is often used to treat osteoarthritis pain. While NSAIDs should be used cautiously in hypertension, mild hypertension is not an absolute contraindication compared to an active bleeding ulcer. 
</details>

---

### Question 203
A client receiving oral ferrous sulfate for iron deficiency anemia reports having dark, greenish-black stools for the past two days. What is the nurse's best action in response to this finding?

A) Instruct the client to stop taking the medication immediately.
B) Send a stool sample to the laboratory for occult blood testing.
C) Document the finding as an expected side effect of the medication.
D) Administer a PRN dose of an antidiarrheal medication.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens 3: EXPECTED VS UNEXPECTED</strong>
Dark, greenish-black stools are an expected, harmless side effect of oral iron supplementation (ferrous sulfate). The nurse should document the finding and reassure the client that this is normal. There is no need to stop the medication, test for occult blood (unless other signs of GI bleeding are present), or administer antidiarrheals, as iron is actually more likely to cause constipation.
</details>

---

### Question 204
The nurse is providing discharge teaching to a client newly prescribed sublingual nitroglycerin for stable angina. Which statement by the client indicates a correct understanding of how to take this medication?

A) "I will swallow the tablet whole with a full glass of water."
B) "I should chew the tablet if my chest pain feels very severe."
C) "I can take this medication with a small glass of grapefruit juice."
D) "I will place the tablet under my tongue and let it dissolve completely."

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> D

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens 4: CLIENT TEACHING</strong>
Sublingual medications are designed to be absorbed directly through the highly vascular mucous membranes under the tongue, allowing for rapid onset into the systemic circulation. Swallowing or chewing the tablet destroys its efficacy due to the liver's first-pass metabolism. Grapefruit juice is irrelevant to sublingual absorption but is contraindicated with many other oral cardiovascular drugs (like statins or calcium channel blockers).
</details>

---

### Question 205
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion for a pulmonary embolism. The nurse reviews the morning laboratory results and notes an activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) of 65 seconds. The normal control value is 30 seconds. How should the nurse interpret this result?

A) The client's blood is clotting too quickly, requiring a dosage increase.
B) The client is experiencing severe heparin toxicity, requiring an antidote.
C) The result is within the expected therapeutic range for heparin therapy.
D) The result is critically low, and the infusion must be stopped immediately.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer:</strong> C

<strong>Rationale:</strong>
<strong>Lens 5: DATA INTERPRETATION</strong>
The therapeutic goal for a client on a continuous heparin infusion is an aPTT of 1.5 to 2.5 times the normal control value. If the control is 30 seconds, the therapeutic range is roughly 45 to 75 seconds.
